And I can see the person’s face change, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t, and then I realize I’ve wandered way off from what they asked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to do it. I’m trying to answer, but it’s like my words take the long way around and then don’t come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high or full of energy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “high” way, if you mean like suddenly feeling great or unstoppable. I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nEnergy is kind of hard for me to judge because some days I can get busy with little things and look like I’m active, but it doesn’t feel like this big surge or anything. It’s more like I’m just trying to get through what’s in front of me. I haven’t really noticed myself feeling unusually energized or elevated, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Not like a whole conversation exactly, or not usually. It’s more like when the shop gets really quiet, especially if I’m back near the benches or the polishing room, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman kind of muttering my name. Not loud, not like someone standing right next to me talking normally, but clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange because I’ll think, “Well, maybe someone came in,” or maybe one of the machines made a noise in a certain way, but then I go check the polishing room and there’s no one there. It feels real in the moment, real enough that I don’t just ignore it.\n\nThere’s also this smell sometimes — I know you asked about voices, but it’s kind of similar in how it makes me check — like burning metal, that hot torch smell, when the torches are off and nothing should be heating. I’ll walk around making sure something isn’t on or dangerous. So… yes, I guess I do hear something sometimes when no one else is around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that anyone has ever told me, and I’ve never had one of those episodes where I fell down shaking or woke up with people around me saying something happened.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s anything that would count as one, because sometimes medical words mean something more specific than what I picture. But as far as I know, no, I haven’t had a seizure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well, or I was up too late fussing with something, but the next day I don’t feel rested from it. I usually feel worn down or foggy, like I’m dragging myself along.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only need a couple hours and then wake up feeling completely fine or extra good, if that’s what you mean. For me, less sleep usually just feels like less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. It’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not waking up crying every day or anything like that. It’s more like this weight is already there before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAnd then I’ll go through the morning motions — making coffee, getting dressed, thinking about what needs to be done — but there’s this dull feeling over everything. Like even the light in the kitchen seems too gray, or the rooms feel emptied out somehow. It tends to follow me into the day, so by the time I’m at the shop I’m already trying to work around it rather than starting fresh.\n\nSo… yes, depressed most of the day, most days, that sounds right.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice at times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were times like that. It’s a little hard to separate out now because the depression feels like it’s been coloring everything for a while, but I do remember a stretch before it really settled in.\n\nI was still running my classes at the shop then, and I was out in the garden pretty regularly, so I wasn’t feeling this same heavy, sunken thing all day. But even during that period, for several weeks, I kept hearing noises from the polishing room when I didn’t think anyone was back there. It would make me stop what I was doing and listen, and then I’d get this feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were noticing something or keeping an eye on me.\n\nSo, yes, the voice or the noises — however to describe it — weren’t only when I was feeling depressed. There was that earlier stretch when I was still functioning more normally, doing classes and gardening, and it was happening then too.\n\nDoctor: Did these unusual experiences begin suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was over just a few days. It doesn’t feel that clean in my memory. It’s more like… I started noticing little things and then, after a while, I realized I was paying attention to them all the time.\n\nI don’t remember waking up one week and everything being suddenly different. It felt more gradual than that, I think. But I also wasn’t keeping track at the time, so it’s hard to say exactly when it “began,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. I mean, I’ve bumped my head like anyone does — on cabinet doors, or leaning into a workbench and misjudging it, little things like that — but nothing that I’d call a real head injury before all this started.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a fall where I blacked out or had to go to the hospital, at least not in connection with when these experiences began. If there was something minor, I honestly wouldn’t have thought much of it at the time. But no, nothing stands out to me as a head injury that came before it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to admit, because so much of who I am has always been tied up in the work.\n\nI used to really love hunting for old clasps and little findings — I could spend hours looking through boxes at estate sales or suppliers, imagining what I might do with them. And I used to get excited planning the small jewelry classes, thinking about what people could make, setting out the trays, picking stones that would catch the light nicely. That used to feel satisfying to me, like it had a purpose.\n\nLately, though, I mostly just stand there or sit at the bench and stare at the trays. I’ll look at the clasps or the beads or the bits of silver, and instead of ideas coming, it just feels like… none of it is worth starting. Like even choosing one thing to work on feels too far away somehow. I still know, in my head, that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t really feel that pull toward it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that customers were watching you been going on for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s hard for me to pin it down neatly, because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress or me being too sensitive at the shop, but when I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like customers were watching me started around… about a year and eight months ago.\n\nIt wasn’t every second at the beginning, but that’s about when I remember it becoming something I couldn’t just brush off. I started noticing it and then keeping track of it in my head, and over time it just became part of what I was dealing with. So yes, much longer than a month.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt watched, but you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. That’s part of why I get confused trying to explain the timeline, because the watching feeling and the voice—or the noises, especially from the polishing room—were there before I really felt sunk down the way I did later.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still functioning more like myself, at least on the outside. I was still running the jewelry classes, still setting things up for people, still trying to keep the shop moving. I was also still gardening some then, which is usually one of the things I can do when I have a little energy. I wouldn’t say I felt wonderful, but I don’t think I was depressed in the same way yet. I was still doing things.\n\nBut even during that time, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room and getting uneasy about customers watching the business. It was like I could be teaching or arranging supplies and still have part of my mind pulled toward, “Did I hear something back there?” or “Are they paying too much attention to what I’m doing?” So yes, there was at least a couple of weeks—really more than that—where those experiences were happening before the depression really settled in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the whole time since these problems began, have you been depressed for most of that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have been depressed for most of it. Not every single minute in exactly the same way, but when I look back over the whole stretch, the bleakness has been there much more often than not.\n\nIt hasn’t felt like just a couple of bad weeks and then I bounced back. It’s more like it became part of the background of my days. I’d wake up already feeling heavy, and then I wouldn’t sleep well at night, so the next day would start with that same worn-down feeling. My appetite has been low a lot of the time too, and then I feel guilty about that, and guilty about not keeping up with things the way I think I should.\n\nSo yes… for most of the time since this all began, the low mood, the poor sleep, the not wanting to eat much, and that guilty feeling have been part of my daily life. It’s been more steady than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: At any point since this started, did the voice or the feeling of being watched continue for at least two weeks when you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. I know I already sort of mentioned it, but that part stands out to me because it was before I really felt like the depression had settled over everything.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still doing my normal things, more or less. I was still running the jewelry classes, getting the materials ready, helping people with their work, and I was still gardening some at home. So I wasn’t in that heavier low place yet, not in the way it became later.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room, or thinking I heard something, and I’d get pulled into worrying about it. And I had that feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were paying attention in a way that didn’t feel right. So yes, the voice or noises and that watched feeling continued for at least two weeks when I wasn’t really depressed yet.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still find yourself speaking very little or seeming emotionally blank to other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as emotionally blank, exactly. Other people might see me differently, I guess, but nobody has really said it to me in those words.\n\nI can get quiet, especially if I’m trying to concentrate or if I don’t know what to say, but that doesn’t necessarily feel like I’m empty or not feeling anything. A lot of the time there’s plenty going on inside, even if it doesn’t show well on my face. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that as a separate thing when I’m not depressed. What do you mean by emotionally blank—like not reacting at all?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, I don’t think anyone has really told me that, not directly. I mean, people have said I seem tired, or quiet, or like I’m “somewhere else,” but I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks blank or that I don’t show emotion.

It’s hard for me to judge from the inside, because I might feel like I’m reacting normally, but maybe it doesn’t show much. I know when I’m focused—like if I’m working on something small or detailed—I can probably look pretty still. But I don’t know if that’s the same as looking emotionally blank.

So… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed that, and people haven’t really told me that in those words.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is… it’s getting hard to answer people in a normal way. Like at work, someone will ask me something simple — “When will my ring repair be ready?” — and I know the answer should just be, you know, “Tuesday” or “I’ll call you tomorrow,” something like that.\n\nBut instead I start explaining the invoice, and then I’m thinking about the little envelope the receipt goes in, and then somehow I’m talking about garden soil, because the color reminds me of something, and then I’m on mortgage papers or some form I misplaced at home. I’m trying to answer, but it’s like my words take the long way around and then don’t come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high or full of energy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “high” way, if you mean like suddenly feeling great or unstoppable. I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nEnergy is kind of hard for me to judge because some days I can get busy with little things and look like I’m active, but it doesn’t feel like this big surge or anything. It’s more like I’m just trying to get through what’s in front of me. I haven’t really noticed myself feeling unusually energized or elevated, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Not like a whole conversation exactly, or not usually. It’s more like when the shop gets really quiet, especially if I’m back near the benches or the polishing room, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman kind of muttering my name. Not loud, not like someone standing right next to me talking normally, but clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange because I’ll think, “Well, maybe someone came in,” or maybe one of the machines made a noise in a certain way, but then I go check the polishing room and there’s no one there. It feels real in the moment, real enough that I don’t just ignore it.\n\nThere’s also this smell sometimes — I know you asked about voices, but it’s kind of similar in how it makes me check — like burning metal, that hot torch smell, when the torches are off and nothing should be heating. I’ll walk around making sure something isn’t on or dangerous. So… yes, I guess I do hear something sometimes when no one else is around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that anyone has ever told me, and I’ve never had one of those episodes where I fell down shaking or woke up with people around me saying something happened.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s anything that would count as one, because sometimes medical words mean something more specific than what I picture. But as far as I know, no, I haven’t had a seizure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well, or I was up too late fussing with something, but the next day I don’t feel rested from it. I usually feel worn down or foggy, like I’m dragging myself along.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only need a couple hours and then wake up feeling completely fine or extra good, if that’s what you mean. For me, less sleep usually just feels like less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. It’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not waking up crying every day or anything like that. It’s more like this weight is already there before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAnd then I’ll go through the morning motions — making coffee, getting dressed, thinking about what needs to be done — but there’s this dull feeling over everything. Like even the light in the kitchen seems too gray, or the rooms feel emptied out somehow. It tends to follow me into the day, so by the time I’m at the shop I’m already trying to work around it rather than starting fresh.\n\nSo… yes, depressed most of the day, most days, that sounds right.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice at times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were times like that. It’s a little hard to separate out now because the depression feels like it’s been coloring everything for a while, but I do remember a stretch before it really settled in.\n\nI was still running my classes at the shop then, and I was out in the garden pretty regularly, so I wasn’t feeling this same heavy, sunken thing all day. But even during that period, for several weeks, I kept hearing noises from the polishing room when I didn’t think anyone was back there. It would make me stop what I was doing and listen, and then I’d get this feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were noticing something or keeping an eye on me.\n\nSo, yes, the voice or the noises — however to describe it — weren’t only when I was feeling depressed. There was that earlier stretch when I was still functioning more normally, doing classes and gardening, and it was happening then too.\n\nDoctor: Did these unusual experiences begin suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was over just a few days. It doesn’t feel that clean in my memory. It’s more like… I started noticing little things and then, after a while, I realized I was paying attention to them all the time.\n\nI don’t remember waking up one week and everything being suddenly different. It felt more gradual than that, I think. But I also wasn’t keeping track at the time, so it’s hard to say exactly when it “began,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. I mean, I’ve bumped my head like anyone does — on cabinet doors, or leaning into a workbench and misjudging it, little things like that — but nothing that I’d call a real head injury before all this started.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a fall where I blacked out or had to go to the hospital, at least not in connection with when these experiences began. If there was something minor, I honestly wouldn’t have thought much of it at the time. But no, nothing stands out to me as a head injury that came before it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to admit, because so much of who I am has always been tied up in the work.\n\nI used to really love hunting for old clasps and little findings — I could spend hours looking through boxes at estate sales or suppliers, imagining what I might do with them. And I used to get excited planning the small jewelry classes, thinking about what people could make, setting out the trays, picking stones that would catch the light nicely. That used to feel satisfying to me, like it had a purpose.\n\nLately, though, I mostly just stand there or sit at the bench and stare at the trays. I’ll look at the clasps or the beads or the bits of silver, and instead of ideas coming, it just feels like… none of it is worth starting. Like even choosing one thing to work on feels too far away somehow. I still know, in my head, that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t really feel that pull toward it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that customers were watching you been going on for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s hard for me to pin it down neatly, because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress or me being too sensitive at the shop, but when I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like customers were watching me started around… about a year and eight months ago.\n\nIt wasn’t every second at the beginning, but that’s about when I remember it becoming something I couldn’t just brush off. I started noticing it and then keeping track of it in my head, and over time it just became part of what I was dealing with. So yes, much longer than a month.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt watched, but you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. That’s part of why I get confused trying to explain the timeline, because the watching feeling and the voice—or the noises, especially from the polishing room—were there before I really felt sunk down the way I did later.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still functioning more like myself, at least on the outside. I was still running the jewelry classes, still setting things up for people, still trying to keep the shop moving. I was also still gardening some then, which is usually one of the things I can do when I have a little energy. I wouldn’t say I felt wonderful, but I don’t think I was depressed in the same way yet. I was still doing things.\n\nBut even during that time, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room and getting uneasy about customers watching the business. It was like I could be teaching or arranging supplies and still have part of my mind pulled toward, “Did I hear something back there?” or “Are they paying too much attention to what I’m doing?” So yes, there was at least a couple of weeks—really more than that—where those experiences were happening before the depression really settled in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the whole time since these problems began, have you been depressed for most of that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have been depressed for most of it. Not every single minute in exactly the same way, but when I look back over the whole stretch, the bleakness has been there much more often than not.\n\nIt hasn’t felt like just a couple of bad weeks and then I bounced back. It’s more like it became part of the background of my days. I’d wake up already feeling heavy, and then I wouldn’t sleep well at night, so the next day would start with that same worn-down feeling. My appetite has been low a lot of the time too, and then I feel guilty about that, and guilty about not keeping up with things the way I think I should.\n\nSo yes… for most of the time since this all began, the low mood, the poor sleep, the not wanting to eat much, and that guilty feeling have been part of my daily life. It’s been more steady than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: At any point since this started, did the voice or the feeling of being watched continue for at least two weeks when you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. I know I already sort of mentioned it, but that part stands out to me because it was before I really felt like the depression had settled over everything.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still doing my normal things, more or less. I was still running the jewelry classes, getting the materials ready, helping people with their work, and I was still gardening some at home. So I wasn’t in that heavier low place yet, not in the way it became later.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room, or thinking I heard something, and I’d get pulled into worrying about it. And I had that feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were paying attention in a way that didn’t feel right. So yes, the voice or noises and that watched feeling continued for at least two weeks when I wasn’t really depressed yet.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still find yourself speaking very little or seeming emotionally blank to other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as emotionally blank, exactly. Other people might see me differently, I guess, but nobody has really said it to me in those words.\n\nI can get quiet, especially if I’m trying to concentrate or if I don’t know what to say, but that doesn’t necessarily feel like I’m empty or not feeling anything. A lot of the time there’s plenty going on inside, even if it doesn’t show well on my face. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that as a separate thing when I’m not depressed. What do you mean by emotionally blank—like not reacting at all?\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do other people still tell you that your face looks blank or that you show very little emotion?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone has really told me that, not directly. I mean, people have said I seem tired, or quiet, or like I’m “somewhere else,” but I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks blank or that I don’t show emotion.\n\nIt’s hard for me to judge from the inside, because I might feel like I’m reacting normally, but maybe it doesn’t show much. I know when I’m focused—like if I’m working on something small or detailed—I can probably look pretty still. But I don’t know if that’s the same as looking emotionally blank.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed that, and people haven’t really told me that in those words.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I don’t know that I’d call it trouble starting things when I’m not depressed. I mean, I can procrastinate like anyone, especially if something feels tedious or if there are too many small steps, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a real separate problem.

Same with things around the house — I might put something off, but not in that stuck, can’t-move kind of way.

So I guess… not really, or at least not that I’ve recognized. I’d want to know what level of trouble you mean, because everyday motivation goes up and down for me, but I don’t think people would say I just stop keeping up when I’m not depressed.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is… it’s getting hard to answer people in a normal way. Like at work, someone will ask me something simple — “When will my ring repair be ready?” — and I know the answer should just be, you know, “Tuesday” or “I’ll call you tomorrow,” something like that.\n\nBut instead I start explaining the invoice, and then I’m thinking about the little envelope the receipt goes in, and then somehow I’m talking about garden soil, because the color reminds me of something, and then I’m on mortgage papers or some form I misplaced at home. And I can see the person’s face change, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t, and then I realize I’ve wandered way off from what they asked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to do it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get through what’s in front of me. I haven’t really noticed myself feeling unusually energized or elevated, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Not like a whole conversation exactly, or not usually. It feels real in the moment, real enough that I don’t just ignore it.\n\nThere’s also this smell sometimes — I know you asked about voices, but it’s kind of similar in how it makes me check — like burning metal, that hot torch smell, when the torches are off and nothing should be heating. I’ll walk around making sure something isn’t on or dangerous. So… yes, I guess I do hear something sometimes when no one else is around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that anyone has ever told me, and I’ve never had one of those episodes where I fell down shaking or woke up with people around me saying something happened.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s anything that would count as one, because sometimes medical words mean something more specific than what I picture. But as far as I know, no, I haven’t had a seizure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well, or I was up too late fussing with something, but the next day I don’t feel rested from it. I usually feel worn down or foggy, like I’m dragging myself along.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only need a couple hours and then wake up feeling completely fine or extra good, if that’s what you mean. For me, less sleep usually just feels like less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. It’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not waking up crying every day or anything like that. It’s more like this weight is already there before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAnd then I’ll go through the morning motions — making coffee, getting dressed, thinking about what needs to be done — but there’s this dull feeling over everything. Like even the light in the kitchen seems too gray, or the rooms feel emptied out somehow. It tends to follow me into the day, so by the time I’m at the shop I’m already trying to work around it rather than starting fresh.\n\nSo… yes, depressed most of the day, most days, that sounds right.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice at times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were times like that. It’s a little hard to separate out now because the depression feels like it’s been coloring everything for a while, but I do remember a stretch before it really settled in.\n\nI was still running my classes at the shop then, and I was out in the garden pretty regularly, so I wasn’t feeling this same heavy, sunken thing all day. But even during that period, for several weeks, I kept hearing noises from the polishing room when I didn’t think anyone was back there. It would make me stop what I was doing and listen, and then I’d get this feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were noticing something or keeping an eye on me.\n\nSo, yes, the voice or the noises — however to describe it — weren’t only when I was feeling depressed. There was that earlier stretch when I was still functioning more normally, doing classes and gardening, and it was happening then too.\n\nDoctor: Did these unusual experiences begin suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was over just a few days. It doesn’t feel that clean in my memory. It’s more like… I started noticing little things and then, after a while, I realized I was paying attention to them all the time.\n\nI don’t remember waking up one week and everything being suddenly different. It felt more gradual than that, I think. But I also wasn’t keeping track at the time, so it’s hard to say exactly when it “began,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. I mean, I’ve bumped my head like anyone does — on cabinet doors, or leaning into a workbench and misjudging it, little things like that — but nothing that I’d call a real head injury before all this started.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a fall where I blacked out or had to go to the hospital, at least not in connection with when these experiences began. If there was something minor, I honestly wouldn’t have thought much of it at the time. But no, nothing stands out to me as a head injury that came before it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to admit, because so much of who I am has always been tied up in the work.\n\nI used to really love hunting for old clasps and little findings — I could spend hours looking through boxes at estate sales or suppliers, imagining what I might do with them. And I used to get excited planning the small jewelry classes, thinking about what people could make, setting out the trays, picking stones that would catch the light nicely. That used to feel satisfying to me, like it had a purpose.\n\nLately, though, I mostly just stand there or sit at the bench and stare at the trays. I’ll look at the clasps or the beads or the bits of silver, and instead of ideas coming, it just feels like… none of it is worth starting. Like even choosing one thing to work on feels too far away somehow. I still know, in my head, that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t really feel that pull toward it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that customers were watching you been going on for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s hard for me to pin it down neatly, because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress or me being too sensitive at the shop, but when I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like customers were watching me started around… about a year and eight months ago.\n\nIt wasn’t every second at the beginning, but that’s about when I remember it becoming something I couldn’t just brush off. I started noticing it and then keeping track of it in my head, and over time it just became part of what I was dealing with. So yes, much longer than a month.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt watched, but you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. That’s part of why I get confused trying to explain the timeline, because the watching feeling and the voice—or the noises, especially from the polishing room—were there before I really felt sunk down the way I did later.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still functioning more like myself, at least on the outside. I was still running the jewelry classes, still setting things up for people, still trying to keep the shop moving. I was also still gardening some then, which is usually one of the things I can do when I have a little energy. I wouldn’t say I felt wonderful, but I don’t think I was depressed in the same way yet. Not loud, not like someone standing right next to me talking normally, but clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange because I’ll think, “Well, maybe someone came in,” or maybe one of the machines made a noise in a certain way, but then I go check the polishing room and there’s no one there. It feels real in the moment, real enough that I don’t just ignore it.\n\nThere’s also this smell sometimes — I know you asked about voices, but it’s kind of similar in how it makes me check — like burning metal, that hot torch smell, when the torches are off and nothing should be heating. I’ll walk around making sure something isn’t on or dangerous. So… yes, I guess I do hear something sometimes when no one else is around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that anyone has ever told me, and I’ve never had one of those episodes where I fell down shaking or woke up with people around me saying something happened.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s anything that would count as one, because sometimes medical words mean something more specific than what I picture. But as far as I know, no, I haven’t had a seizure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well, or I was up too late fussing with something, but the next day I don’t feel rested from it. I usually feel worn down or foggy, like I’m dragging myself along.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only need a couple hours and then wake up feeling completely fine or extra good, if that’s what you mean. For me, less sleep usually just feels like less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. It’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not waking up crying every day or anything like that. It’s more like this weight is already there before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAnd then I’ll go through the morning motions — making coffee, getting dressed, thinking about what needs to be done — but there’s this dull feeling over everything. Like even the light in the kitchen seems too gray, or the rooms feel emptied out somehow. It tends to follow me into the day, so by the time I’m at the shop I’m already trying to work around it rather than starting fresh.\n\nSo… yes, depressed most of the day, most days, that sounds right.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice at times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were times like that. It’s a little hard to separate out now because the depression feels like it’s been coloring everything for a while, but I do remember a stretch before it really settled in.\n\nI was still running my classes at the shop then, and I was out in the garden pretty regularly, so I wasn’t feeling this same heavy, sunken thing all day. But even during that period, for several weeks, I kept hearing noises from the polishing room when I didn’t think anyone was back there. It would make me stop what I was doing and listen, and then I’d get this feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were noticing something or keeping an eye on me.\n\nSo, yes, the voice or the noises — however to describe it — weren’t only when I was feeling depressed. There was that earlier stretch when I was still functioning more normally, doing classes and gardening, and it was happening then too.\n\nDoctor: Did these unusual experiences begin suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was over just a few days. It doesn’t feel that clean in my memory. It’s more like… I started noticing little things and then, after a while, I realized I was paying attention to them all the time.\n\nI don’t remember waking up one week and everything being suddenly different. It felt more gradual than that, I think. But I also wasn’t keeping track at the time, so it’s hard to say exactly when it “began,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. I mean, I’ve bumped my head like anyone does — on cabinet doors, or leaning into a workbench and misjudging it, little things like that — but nothing that I’d call a real head injury before all this started.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a fall where I blacked out or had to go to the hospital, at least not in connection with when these experiences began. If there was something minor, I honestly wouldn’t have thought much of it at the time. But no, nothing stands out to me as a head injury that came before it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to admit, because so much of who I am has always been tied up in the work.\n\nI used to really love hunting for old clasps and little findings — I could spend hours looking through boxes at estate sales or suppliers, imagining what I might do with them. And I used to get excited planning the small jewelry classes, thinking about what people could make, setting out the trays, picking stones that would catch the light nicely. That used to feel satisfying to me, like it had a purpose.\n\nLately, though, I mostly just stand there or sit at the bench and stare at the trays. I’ll look at the clasps or the beads or the bits of silver, and instead of ideas coming, it just feels like… none of it is worth starting. Like even choosing one thing to work on feels too far away somehow. I still know, in my head, that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t really feel that pull toward it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that customers were watching you been going on for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s hard for me to pin it down neatly, because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress or me being too sensitive at the shop, but when I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like customers were watching me started around… about a year and eight months ago.\n\nIt wasn’t every second at the beginning, but that’s about when I remember it becoming something I couldn’t just brush off. I started noticing it and then keeping track of it in my head, and over time it just became part of what I was dealing with. So yes, much longer than a month.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt watched, but you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. That’s part of why I get confused trying to explain the timeline, because the watching feeling and the voice—or the noises, especially from the polishing room—were there before I really felt sunk down the way I did later.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still functioning more like myself, at least on the outside. I was still running the jewelry classes, still setting things up for people, still trying to keep the shop moving. I was also still gardening some then, which is usually one of the things I can do when I have a little energy. I wouldn’t say I felt wonderful, but I don’t think I was depressed in the same way yet. I was still doing things.\n\nBut even during that time, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room and getting uneasy about customers watching the business. It was like I could be teaching or arranging supplies and still have part of my mind pulled toward, “Did I hear something back there?” or “Are they paying too much attention to what I’m doing?” So yes, there was at least a couple of weeks—really more than that—where those experiences were happening before the depression really settled in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the whole time since these problems began, have you been depressed for most of that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have been depressed for most of it. Not every single minute in exactly the same way, but when I look back over the whole stretch, the bleakness has been there much more often than not.\n\nIt hasn’t felt like just a couple of bad weeks and then I bounced back. It’s more like it became part of the background of my days. I’d wake up already feeling heavy, and then I wouldn’t sleep well at night, so the next day would start with that same worn-down feeling. My appetite has been low a lot of the time too, and then I feel guilty about that, and guilty about not keeping up with things the way I think I should.\n\nSo yes… for most of the time since this all began, the low mood, the poor sleep, the not wanting to eat much, and that guilty feeling have been part of my daily life. It’s been more steady than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: At any point since this started, did the voice or the feeling of being watched continue for at least two weeks when you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. I know I already sort of mentioned it, but that part stands out to me because it was before I really felt like the depression had settled over everything.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still doing my normal things, more or less. I was still running the jewelry classes, getting the materials ready, helping people with their work, and I was still gardening some at home. So I wasn’t in that heavier low place yet, not in the way it became later.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room, or thinking I heard something, and I’d get pulled into worrying about it. And I had that feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were paying attention in a way that didn’t feel right. So yes, the voice or noises and that watched feeling continued for at least two weeks when I wasn’t really depressed yet.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still find yourself speaking very little or seeming emotionally blank to other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as emotionally blank, exactly. Other people might see me differently, I guess, but nobody has really said it to me in those words.\n\nI can get quiet, especially if I’m trying to concentrate or if I don’t know what to say, but that doesn’t necessarily feel like I’m empty or not feeling anything. A lot of the time there’s plenty going on inside, even if it doesn’t show well on my face. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that as a separate thing when I’m not depressed. What do you mean by emotionally blank—like not reacting at all?\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do other people still tell you that your face looks blank or that you show very little emotion?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone has really told me that, not directly. I mean, people have said I seem tired, or quiet, or like I’m “somewhere else,” but I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks blank or that I don’t show emotion.\n\nIt’s hard for me to judge from the inside, because I might feel like I’m reacting normally, but maybe it doesn’t show much. I know when I’m focused—like if I’m working on something small or detailed—I can probably look pretty still. But I don’t know if that’s the same as looking emotionally blank.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed that, and people haven’t really told me that in those words.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still have trouble getting yourself to start or keep up with everyday activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it trouble starting things when I’m not depressed. I mean, I can procrastinate like anyone, especially if something feels tedious or if there are too many small steps, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a real separate problem.\n\nWhen I’m doing okay mood-wise, I usually can get myself moving with the ordinary things. If there’s a class to prepare for or materials that need sorting, I may grumble about it a little, but I can still do it. Same with things around the house — I might put something off, but not in that stuck, can’t-move kind of way.\n\nSo I guess… not really, or at least not that I’ve recognized. I’d want to know what level of trouble you mean, because everyday motivation goes up and down for me, but I don’t think people would say I just stop keeping up when I’m not depressed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that one, yes. I mean, I don’t always realize it while I’m doing it, but it happens enough that it’s part of why I came in.

Like at the shop, someone will ask me something very simple, like, “When will this ring repair be ready?” And instead of just saying, “Thursday afternoon,” my answer starts going sideways. I’ll start explaining that I have to check the invoice, and then I’m talking about where the invoice folder got put, and somehow that reminds me of the garden soil I bought because the receipt was in the same bag, and then I’m thinking about mortgage papers on the kitchen table because I’ve been trying to keep paperwork straight… and by then the person is just standing there looking at me like they don’t know what question I’m answering anymore.

It’s embarrassing, because in my head there is a connection. It doesn’t feel random to me at the time. Each thing sort of hooks onto the next thing. But I can see people’s faces change, like they’re trying to follow and then they just can’t. Sometimes I notice halfway through and try to circle back, but then I’m not sure what the original question was, or I answer it after giving them this whole long detour they never asked for.

So yes, even when I’m not feeling especially depressed, that wandering-off thing can still happen.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is… it’s getting hard to answer people in a normal way. Like at work, someone will ask me something simple — “When will my ring repair be ready?” — and I know the answer should just be, you know, “Tuesday” or “I’ll call you tomorrow,” something like that.\n\nBut instead I start explaining the invoice, and then I’m thinking about the little envelope the receipt goes in, and then somehow I’m talking about garden soil, because the color reminds me of something, and then I’m on mortgage papers or some form I misplaced at home. And I can see the person’s face change, like they’re trying to follow me and they can’t, and then I realize I’ve wandered way off from what they asked.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to do it. I’m trying to answer, but it’s like my words take the long way around and then don’t come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high or full of energy lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that “high” way, if you mean like suddenly feeling great or unstoppable. I wouldn’t describe it that way.\n\nEnergy is kind of hard for me to judge because some days I can get busy with little things and look like I’m active, but it doesn’t feel like this big surge or anything. It’s more like I’m just trying to get through what’s in front of me. I haven’t really noticed myself feeling unusually energized or elevated, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Not like a whole conversation exactly, or not usually. It’s more like when the shop gets really quiet, especially if I’m back near the benches or the polishing room, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman kind of muttering my name. Not loud, not like someone standing right next to me talking normally, but clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd I know that sounds strange because I’ll think, “Well, maybe someone came in,” or maybe one of the machines made a noise in a certain way, but then I go check the polishing room and there’s no one there. It feels real in the moment, real enough that I don’t just ignore it.\n\nThere’s also this smell sometimes — I know you asked about voices, but it’s kind of similar in how it makes me check — like burning metal, that hot torch smell, when the torches are off and nothing should be heating. I’ll walk around making sure something isn’t on or dangerous. So… yes, I guess I do hear something sometimes when no one else is around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not that anyone has ever told me, and I’ve never had one of those episodes where I fell down shaking or woke up with people around me saying something happened.\n\nI’m trying to think if there’s anything that would count as one, because sometimes medical words mean something more specific than what I picture. But as far as I know, no, I haven’t had a seizure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you needed much less sleep than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well, or I was up too late fussing with something, but the next day I don’t feel rested from it. I usually feel worn down or foggy, like I’m dragging myself along.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only need a couple hours and then wake up feeling completely fine or extra good, if that’s what you mean. For me, less sleep usually just feels like less sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Most mornings I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has already drained out of the house before I even get to the shop. It’s not always dramatic, exactly. I’m not waking up crying every day or anything like that. It’s more like this weight is already there before I’ve even put my feet on the floor.\n\nAnd then I’ll go through the morning motions — making coffee, getting dressed, thinking about what needs to be done — but there’s this dull feeling over everything. Like even the light in the kitchen seems too gray, or the rooms feel emptied out somehow. It tends to follow me into the day, so by the time I’m at the shop I’m already trying to work around it rather than starting fresh.\n\nSo… yes, depressed most of the day, most days, that sounds right.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice at times when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were times like that. It’s a little hard to separate out now because the depression feels like it’s been coloring everything for a while, but I do remember a stretch before it really settled in.\n\nI was still running my classes at the shop then, and I was out in the garden pretty regularly, so I wasn’t feeling this same heavy, sunken thing all day. But even during that period, for several weeks, I kept hearing noises from the polishing room when I didn’t think anyone was back there. It would make me stop what I was doing and listen, and then I’d get this feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were noticing something or keeping an eye on me.\n\nSo, yes, the voice or the noises — however to describe it — weren’t only when I was feeling depressed. There was that earlier stretch when I was still functioning more normally, doing classes and gardening, and it was happening then too.\n\nDoctor: Did these unusual experiences begin suddenly, over a few days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it was over just a few days. It doesn’t feel that clean in my memory. It’s more like… I started noticing little things and then, after a while, I realized I was paying attention to them all the time.\n\nI don’t remember waking up one week and everything being suddenly different. It felt more gradual than that, I think. But I also wasn’t keeping track at the time, so it’s hard to say exactly when it “began,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a head injury before these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember. I mean, I’ve bumped my head like anyone does — on cabinet doors, or leaning into a workbench and misjudging it, little things like that — but nothing that I’d call a real head injury before all this started.\n\nI haven’t had, you know, a fall where I blacked out or had to go to the hospital, at least not in connection with when these experiences began. If there was something minor, I honestly wouldn’t have thought much of it at the time. But no, nothing stands out to me as a head injury that came before it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. That’s actually been one of the harder things to admit, because so much of who I am has always been tied up in the work.\n\nI used to really love hunting for old clasps and little findings — I could spend hours looking through boxes at estate sales or suppliers, imagining what I might do with them. And I used to get excited planning the small jewelry classes, thinking about what people could make, setting out the trays, picking stones that would catch the light nicely. That used to feel satisfying to me, like it had a purpose.\n\nLately, though, I mostly just stand there or sit at the bench and stare at the trays. I’ll look at the clasps or the beads or the bits of silver, and instead of ideas coming, it just feels like… none of it is worth starting. Like even choosing one thing to work on feels too far away somehow. I still know, in my head, that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t really feel that pull toward it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that customers were watching you been going on for more than one month?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than a month. It’s hard for me to pin it down neatly, because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress or me being too sensitive at the shop, but when I think back on it, the voice and that feeling like customers were watching me started around… about a year and eight months ago.\n\nIt wasn’t every second at the beginning, but that’s about when I remember it becoming something I couldn’t just brush off. I started noticing it and then keeping track of it in my head, and over time it just became part of what I was dealing with. So yes, much longer than a month.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing the voice or felt watched, but you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was. That’s part of why I get confused trying to explain the timeline, because the watching feeling and the voice—or the noises, especially from the polishing room—were there before I really felt sunk down the way I did later.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still functioning more like myself, at least on the outside. I was still running the jewelry classes, still setting things up for people, still trying to keep the shop moving. I was also still gardening some then, which is usually one of the things I can do when I have a little energy. I wouldn’t say I felt wonderful, but I don’t think I was depressed in the same way yet. I was still doing things.\n\nBut even during that time, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room and getting uneasy about customers watching the business. It was like I could be teaching or arranging supplies and still have part of my mind pulled toward, “Did I hear something back there?” or “Are they paying too much attention to what I’m doing?” So yes, there was at least a couple of weeks—really more than that—where those experiences were happening before the depression really settled in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the whole time since these problems began, have you been depressed for most of that time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have been depressed for most of it. Not every single minute in exactly the same way, but when I look back over the whole stretch, the bleakness has been there much more often than not.\n\nIt hasn’t felt like just a couple of bad weeks and then I bounced back. It’s more like it became part of the background of my days. I’d wake up already feeling heavy, and then I wouldn’t sleep well at night, so the next day would start with that same worn-down feeling. My appetite has been low a lot of the time too, and then I feel guilty about that, and guilty about not keeping up with things the way I think I should.\n\nSo yes… for most of the time since this all began, the low mood, the poor sleep, the not wanting to eat much, and that guilty feeling have been part of my daily life. It’s been more steady than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: At any point since this started, did the voice or the feeling of being watched continue for at least two weeks when you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that did happen. I know I already sort of mentioned it, but that part stands out to me because it was before I really felt like the depression had settled over everything.\n\nThere was a stretch where I was still doing my normal things, more or less. I was still running the jewelry classes, getting the materials ready, helping people with their work, and I was still gardening some at home. So I wasn’t in that heavier low place yet, not in the way it became later.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I kept hearing things from the polishing room, or thinking I heard something, and I’d get pulled into worrying about it. And I had that feeling that customers were watching the business too closely, like they were paying attention in a way that didn’t feel right. So yes, the voice or noises and that watched feeling continued for at least two weeks when I wasn’t really depressed yet.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still find yourself speaking very little or seeming emotionally blank to other people?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as emotionally blank, exactly. Other people might see me differently, I guess, but nobody has really said it to me in those words.\n\nI can get quiet, especially if I’m trying to concentrate or if I don’t know what to say, but that doesn’t necessarily feel like I’m empty or not feeling anything. A lot of the time there’s plenty going on inside, even if it doesn’t show well on my face. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that as a separate thing when I’m not depressed. What do you mean by emotionally blank—like not reacting at all?\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do other people still tell you that your face looks blank or that you show very little emotion?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone has really told me that, not directly. I mean, people have said I seem tired, or quiet, or like I’m “somewhere else,” but I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks blank or that I don’t show emotion.\n\nIt’s hard for me to judge from the inside, because I might feel like I’m reacting normally, but maybe it doesn’t show much. I know when I’m focused—like if I’m working on something small or detailed—I can probably look pretty still. But I don’t know if that’s the same as looking emotionally blank.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed that, and people haven’t really told me that in those words.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still have trouble getting yourself to start or keep up with everyday activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it trouble starting things when I’m not depressed. I mean, I can procrastinate like anyone, especially if something feels tedious or if there are too many small steps, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a real separate problem.\n\nWhen I’m doing okay mood-wise, I usually can get myself moving with the ordinary things. If there’s a class to prepare for or materials that need sorting, I may grumble about it a little, but I can still do it. Same with things around the house — I might put something off, but not in that stuck, can’t-move kind of way.\n\nSo I guess… not really, or at least not that I’ve recognized. I’d want to know what level of trouble you mean, because everyday motivation goes up and down for me, but I don’t think people would say I just stop keeping up when I’m not depressed.\n\nDoctor: When you were not feeling depressed, did you still have the same problem where your answers would wander off track and become hard for other people to follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… that one, yes. I mean, I don’t always realize it while I’m doing it, but it happens enough that it’s part of why I came in.\n\nLike at the shop, someone will ask me something very simple, like, “When will this ring repair be ready?” And instead of just saying, “Thursday afternoon,” my answer starts going sideways. I’ll start explaining that I have to check the invoice, and then I’m talking about where the invoice folder got put, and somehow that reminds me of the garden soil I bought because the receipt was in the same bag, and then I’m thinking about mortgage papers on the kitchen table because I’ve been trying to keep paperwork straight… and by then the person is just standing there looking at me like they don’t know what question I’m answering anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because in my head there is a connection. It doesn’t feel random to me at the time. Each thing sort of hooks onto the next thing. But I can see people’s faces change, like they’re trying to follow and then they just can’t. Sometimes I notice halfway through and try to circle back, but then I’m not sure what the original question was, or I answer it after giving them this whole long detour they never asked for.\n\nSo yes, even when I’m not feeling especially depressed, that wandering-off thing can still happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

{"candidates":["F25.1"],"note":"Psychotic symptoms persisted for >2 weeks outside depressive episodes, depression has been present for most of the illness duration, and there is no evidence of mania/hypomania. This supports schizoaffective disorder, depressive type, over schizophrenia.","is_final":true}

I mean, people have said I seem tired, or quiet, or like I’m “somewhere else,” but I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks blank or that I don’t show emotion.\n\nIt’s hard for me to judge from the inside, because I might feel like I’m reacting normally, but maybe it doesn’t show much. I know when I’m focused—like if I’m working on something small or detailed—I can probably look pretty still. But I don’t know if that’s the same as looking emotionally blank.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed that, and people haven’t really told me that in those words.\n\nDoctor: When you are not feeling depressed, do you still have trouble getting yourself to start or keep up with everyday activities?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it trouble starting things when I’m not depressed. I mean, I can procrastinate like anyone, especially if something feels tedious or if there are too many small steps, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a real separate problem.\n\nWhen I’m doing okay mood-wise, I usually can get myself moving with the ordinary things. If there’s a class to prepare for or materials that need sorting, I may grumble about it a little, but I can still do it. Same with things around the house — I might put something off, but not in that stuck, can’t-move kind of way.\n\nSo I guess… not really, or at least not that I’ve recognized. I’d want to know what level of trouble you mean, because everyday motivation goes up and down for me, but I don’t think people would say I just stop keeping up when I’m not depressed.\n\nDoctor: When you were not feeling depressed, did you still have the same problem where your answers would wander off track and become hard for other people to follow?\n\nPatient: Yes… that one, yes. I mean, I don’t always realize it while I’m doing it, but it happens enough that it’s part of why I came in.\n\nLike at the shop, someone will ask me something very simple, like, “When will this ring repair be ready?” And instead of just saying, “Thursday afternoon,” my answer starts going sideways. I’ll start explaining that I have to check the invoice, and then I’m talking about where the invoice folder got put, and somehow that reminds me of the garden soil I bought because the receipt was in the same bag, and then I’m thinking about mortgage papers on the kitchen table because I’ve been trying to keep paperwork straight… and by then the person is just standing there looking at me like they don’t know what question I’m answering anymore.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because in my head there is a connection. It doesn’t feel random to me at the time. Each thing sort of hooks onto the next thing. But I can see people’s faces change, like they’re trying to follow and then they just can’t. Sometimes I notice halfway through and try to circle back, but then I’m not sure what the original question was, or I answer it after giving them this whole long detour they never asked for.\n\nSo yes, even when I’m not feeling especially depressed, that wandering-off thing can still happen."
  }
